# ColBERTv2: Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction {#colbertv2-effective-and-efficient-retrieval-via-lightweight-late-interaction .ltx_title .ltx_title_document}

::: {#abstract1 .ltx_abstract}
###### Abstract {#abstract .ltx_title .ltx_title_abstract}

Neural information retrieval (IR) has greatly advanced search and other
knowledge-intensive language tasks. While many neural IR methods encode
queries and documents into single-vector representations, late
interaction models produce multi-vector representations at the
granularity of each token and decompose relevance modeling into scalable
token-level computations. This decomposition has been shown to make late
interaction more effective, but it inflates the space footprint of these
models by an order of magnitude. In this work, we introduce ColBERTv2, a
retriever that couples an aggressive residual compression mechanism with
a denoised supervision strategy to simultaneously improve the quality
and space footprint of late interaction. We evaluate ColBERTv2 across a
wide range of benchmarks, establishing state-of-the-art quality within
and outside the training domain while reducing the space footprint of
late interaction models by 6--10$\times$.
:::

:::::::::: {#S1 .section .ltx_section}
## [1 ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_section}Introduction {#introduction .ltx_title .ltx_title_section}

::: {#S1.p1 .ltx_para}
Neural information retrieval (IR) has quickly dominated the search
landscape over the past 2--3 years, dramatically advancing not only
passage and document search [Nogueira and Cho
2019](#bib.bib50){.ltx_ref} but also many knowledge-intensive NLP tasks
like open-domain question answering [Guu et al.
2020](#bib.bib20){.ltx_ref}, multi-hop claim verification [Khattab
et al. 2021a](#bib.bib31){.ltx_ref}, and open-ended generation
[Paranjape et al. 2022](#bib.bib52){.ltx_ref}.
:::

::: {#S1.p2 .ltx_para}
Many neural IR methods follow a [single-vector similarity]{#S1.p2.1.1
.ltx_text .ltx_font_italic} paradigm: a pretrained language model is
used to encode each query and each document into a single
high-dimensional vector, and relevance is modeled as a simple dot
product between both vectors. An alternative is [late
interaction]{#S1.p2.1.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_italic}, introduced in
ColBERT [Khattab and Zaharia 2020](#bib.bib33){.ltx_ref}, where queries
and documents are encoded at a finer-granularity into multi-vector
representations, and relevance is estimated using rich yet scalable
interactions between these two sets of vectors. ColBERT produces an
embedding for every token in the query (and document) and models
relevance as the sum of maximum similarities between each query vector
and all vectors in the document.
:::

::: {#S1.p3 .ltx_para}
By decomposing relevance modeling into token-level computations, late
interaction aims to reduce the burden on the encoder: whereas
single-vector models must capture complex query--document relationships
within one dot product, late interaction encodes meaning at the level of
tokens and delegates query--document matching to the interaction
mechanism. This added expressivity comes at a cost: existing late
interaction systems impose an order-of-magnitude larger [space
footprint]{#S1.p3.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_italic} than single-vector
models, as they must store billions of small vectors for Web-scale
collections. Considering this challenge, it might seem more fruitful to
focus instead on addressing the fragility of single-vector models [Menon
et al. 2022](#bib.bib48){.ltx_ref} by introducing new supervision
paradigms for negative mining [Xiong et al. 2020](#bib.bib67){.ltx_ref},
pretraining [Gao and Callan 2021](#bib.bib16){.ltx_ref}, and
distillation [Qu et al. 2021](#bib.bib53){.ltx_ref}. Indeed, recent
single-vector models with highly-tuned supervision strategies [Ren
et al. 2021b](#bib.bib56){.ltx_ref}; [Formal et al.
2021a](#bib.bib14){.ltx_ref} sometimes perform on-par or even better
than "vanilla" late interaction models, and it is not necessarily clear
whether late interaction architectures---with their fixed token-level
inductive biases---admit similarly large gains from improved
supervision.
:::

::: {#S1.p4 .ltx_para}
In this work, we show that late interaction retrievers naturally produce
lightweight token representations that are amenable to efficient storage
off-the-shelf and that they can benefit drastically from denoised
supervision. We couple those in [ColBERTv2]{#S1.p4.1.1 .ltx_text
.ltx_font_bold},[^1^[[^1^ [1]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_note} Code, models, and
LoTTE data are maintained at
[https://github.com/stanford-futuredata/ColBERT](https://github.com/stanford-futuredata/ColBERT){.ltx_ref
.ltx_url
.ltx_font_typewriter}]{.ltx_note_content}]{.ltx_note_outer}]{#footnote1
.ltx_note .ltx_role_footnote} a new late-interaction retriever that
employs a simple combination of distillation from a cross-encoder and
hard-negative mining (§[[3.2]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Supervision ‣ 3 ColBERTv2 ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref})
to boost quality beyond any existing method, and then uses a [residual
compression]{#S1.p4.1.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_italic} mechanism
(§[[3.3]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S3.SS3 "3.3 Representation ‣ 3 ColBERTv2 ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref})
to reduce the space footprint of late interaction by 6--10$\times$ while
preserving quality. As a result, ColBERTv2 establishes state-of-the-art
retrieval quality both [within]{#S1.p4.1.3 .ltx_text .ltx_font_italic}
and [outside]{#S1.p4.1.4 .ltx_text .ltx_font_italic} its training domain
with a competitive space footprint with typical single-vector models.
:::

::: {#S1.p5 .ltx_para}
When trained on MS MARCO Passage Ranking, ColBERTv2 achieves the highest
MRR@10 of any standalone retriever. In addition to in-domain quality, we
seek a retriever that generalizes "zero-shot" to domain-specific corpora
and long-tail topics, ones that are often under-represented in large
public training sets. To this end, we evaluate ColBERTv2 on a wide array
of [out-of-domain]{#S1.p5.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_italic} benchmarks.
These include three Wikipedia Open-QA retrieval tests and 13 diverse
retrieval and semantic-similarity tasks from BEIR [Thakur et al.
2021](#bib.bib58){.ltx_ref}. In addition, we introduce a new benchmark,
dubbed [LoTTE]{#S1.p5.1.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}, for [Lo]{#S1.p5.1.3
.ltx_text .ltx_underline}ng-[T]{#S1.p5.1.4 .ltx_text .ltx_underline}ail
[T]{#S1.p5.1.5 .ltx_text .ltx_underline}opic-stratified [E]{#S1.p5.1.6
.ltx_text .ltx_underline}valuation for IR that features 12
domain-specific search tests, spanning StackExchange communities and
using queries from GooAQ [Khashabi et al. 2021](#bib.bib30){.ltx_ref}.
LoTTE focuses on relatively long-tail topics in its passages, unlike the
Open-QA tests and many of the BEIR tasks, and evaluates models on their
capacity to answer natural search queries with a practical intent,
unlike many of BEIR's semantic-similarity tasks. On 22 of 28
out-of-domain tests, ColBERTv2 achieves the highest quality,
outperforming the next best retriever by up to 8% relative gain, while
using its compressed representations.
:::

::: {#S1.p6 .ltx_para}
This work makes the following contributions:
:::

::: {#S1.p7 .ltx_para}
1.  [[1.]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S1.I1.i1}

    ::: {#S1.I1.i1.p1 .ltx_para}
    We propose ColBERTv2, a retriever that combines denoised supervision
    and residual compression, leveraging the token-level decomposition
    of late interaction to achieve high robustness with a reduced space
    footprint.
    :::
2.  [[2.]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S1.I1.i2}

    ::: {#S1.I1.i2.p1 .ltx_para}
    We introduce LoTTE, a new resource for out-of-domain evaluation of
    retrievers. LoTTE focuses on natural information-seeking queries
    over long-tail topics, an important yet understudied application
    space.
    :::
3.  [[3.]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_item}]{#S1.I1.i3}

    ::: {#S1.I1.i3.p1 .ltx_para}
    We evaluate ColBERTv2 across a wide range of settings, establishing
    state-of-the-art quality within and outside the training domain.
    :::
:::
::::::::::

::::::::::::::: {#S2 .section .ltx_section}
## [2 ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_section}Background & Related Work {#background-related-work .ltx_title .ltx_title_section}

::::: {#S2.SS1 .section .ltx_subsection}
### [2.1 ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_subsection}Token-Decomposed Scoring in Neural IR {#token-decomposed-scoring-in-neural-ir .ltx_title .ltx_title_subsection}

::: {#S2.SS1.p1 .ltx_para}
Many neural IR approaches encode passages as a single high-dimensional
vector, trading off the higher quality of cross-encoders for improved
efficiency and scalability [Karpukhin et al.
2020](#bib.bib29){.ltx_ref}; [Xiong et al. 2020](#bib.bib67){.ltx_ref};
[Qu et al. 2021](#bib.bib53){.ltx_ref}. ColBERT's [Khattab and Zaharia
2020](#bib.bib33){.ltx_ref} late interaction paradigm addresses this
tradeoff by computing multi-vector embeddings and using a scalable
"MaxSim" operator for retrieval. Several other systems leverage
multi-vector representations, including Poly-encoders [Humeau et al.
2020](#bib.bib23){.ltx_ref}, PreTTR [MacAvaney et al.
2020](#bib.bib44){.ltx_ref}, and MORES [Gao et al.
2020](#bib.bib17){.ltx_ref}, but these target attention-based re-ranking
as opposed to ColBERT's scalable MaxSim end-to-end retrieval.
:::

::: {#S2.SS1.p2 .ltx_para}
ME-BERT [Luan et al. 2021](#bib.bib43){.ltx_ref} generates token-level
document embeddings similar to ColBERT, but retains a single embedding
vector for queries. COIL [Gao et al. 2021](#bib.bib18){.ltx_ref} also
generates token-level document embeddings, but the token interactions
are restricted to lexical matching between query and document terms.
uniCOIL [Lin and Ma 2021](#bib.bib40){.ltx_ref} limits the token
embedding vectors of COIL to a single dimension, reducing them to scalar
weights that extend models like DeepCT [Dai and Callan
2020](#bib.bib11){.ltx_ref} and DeepImpact [Mallia et al.
2021](#bib.bib47){.ltx_ref}. To produce scalar weights, SPLADE [Formal
et al. 2021b](#bib.bib15){.ltx_ref} and SPLADEv2 [Formal et al.
2021a](#bib.bib14){.ltx_ref} produce a sparse vocabulary-level vector
that retains the term-level decomposition of late interaction while
simplifying the storage into one dimension per token. The SPLADE family
also piggybacks on the language modeling capacity acquired by BERT
during pretraining. SPLADEv2 has been shown to be highly effective,
within and across domains, and it is a central point of comparison in
the experiments we report on in this paper.
:::
:::::

:::::: {#S2.SS2 .section .ltx_subsection}
### [2.2 ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_subsection}Vector Compression for Neural IR {#vector-compression-for-neural-ir .ltx_title .ltx_title_subsection}

::: {#S2.SS2.p1 .ltx_para}
There has been a surge of recent interest in compressing representations
for IR. [Izacard et al. 2020](#bib.bib24){.ltx_ref} explore dimension
reduction, product quantization (PQ), and passage filtering for
single-vector retrievers. BPR [Yamada et al.
2021a](#bib.bib68){.ltx_ref} learns to directly hash embeddings to
binary codes using a differentiable [tanh]{#S2.SS2.p1.1.1 .ltx_text
.ltx_font_typewriter} function. JPQ [Zhan et al.
2021a](#bib.bib72){.ltx_ref} and its extension, RepCONC [Zhan et al.
2022](#bib.bib74){.ltx_ref}, use PQ to compress embeddings, and jointly
train the query encoder along with the centroids produced by PQ via a
ranking-oriented loss.
:::

::: {#S2.SS2.p2 .ltx_para}
SDR [Cohen et al. 2021](#bib.bib10){.ltx_ref} uses an autoencoder to
reduce the dimensionality of the contextual embeddings used for
attention-based re-ranking and then applies a quantization scheme for
further compression. DensePhrases [Lee et al.
2021a](#bib.bib35){.ltx_ref} is a system for Open-QA that relies on a
multi-vector encoding of passages, though its search is conducted at the
level of individual vectors and not aggregated with late interaction.
Very recently, [Lee et al. 2021b](#bib.bib36){.ltx_ref} propose a
quantization-aware finetuning method based on PQ to reduce the space
footprint of DensePhrases. While DensePhrases is effective at Open-QA,
its retrieval quality---as measured by top-20 retrieval accuracy on
NaturalQuestions and TriviaQA---is competitive with DPR [Karpukhin
et al. 2020](#bib.bib29){.ltx_ref} and considerably less effective than
ColBERT [Khattab et al. 2021b](#bib.bib32){.ltx_ref}.
:::

::: {#S2.SS2.p3 .ltx_para}
In this work, we focus on late-interaction retrieval and investigate
compression using a residual compression approach that can be applied
off-the-shelf to late interaction models, without special training. We
show in Appendix [[A]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#A1 "Appendix A Analysis of ColBERT’s Semantic Space ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref}
that ColBERT's representations naturally lend themselves to residual
compression. Techniques in the family of residual compression are
well-studied [Barnes et al. 1996](#bib.bib4){.ltx_ref} and have
previously been applied across several domains, including approximate
nearest neighbor search [Wei et al. 2014](#bib.bib64){.ltx_ref}; [Ai
et al. 2017](#bib.bib2){.ltx_ref}, neural network parameter and
activation quantization [Li et al. 2021b](#bib.bib39){.ltx_ref}; [Li
et al. 2021a](#bib.bib38){.ltx_ref}, and distributed deep learning [Chen
et al. 2018](#bib.bib8){.ltx_ref}; [Liu et al.
2020](#bib.bib42){.ltx_ref}. To the best of our knowledge, ColBERTv2 is
the first approach to use residual compression for scalable neural IR.
:::
::::::

:::: {#S2.SS3 .section .ltx_subsection}
### [2.3 ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_subsection}Improving the Quality of Single-Vector Representations {#improving-the-quality-of-single-vector-representations .ltx_title .ltx_title_subsection}

::: {#S2.SS3.p1 .ltx_para}
Instead of compressing multi-vector representations as we do, much
recent work has focused on improving the quality of single-vector
models, which are often very sensitive to the specifics of supervision.
This line of work can be decomposed into three directions: (1)
distillation of more expressive architectures [Hofstätter et al.
2020](#bib.bib21){.ltx_ref}; [Lin et al. 2020](#bib.bib41){.ltx_ref}
including explicit denoising [Qu et al. 2021](#bib.bib53){.ltx_ref};
[Ren et al. 2021b](#bib.bib56){.ltx_ref}, (2) hard negative
sampling [Xiong et al. 2020](#bib.bib67){.ltx_ref}; [Zhan et al.
2020a](#bib.bib75){.ltx_ref}; [Zhan et al. 2021b](#bib.bib73){.ltx_ref},
and (3) improved pretraining [Gao and Callan
2021](#bib.bib16){.ltx_ref}; [Oğuz et al. 2021](#bib.bib51){.ltx_ref}.
We adopt similar techniques to (1) and (2) for ColBERTv2's multi-vector
representations (see §[[3.2]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Supervision ‣ 3 ColBERTv2 ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref}).
:::
::::

::::: {#S2.SS4 .section .ltx_subsection}
### [2.4 ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_subsection}Out-of-Domain Evaluation in IR {#out-of-domain-evaluation-in-ir .ltx_title .ltx_title_subsection}

::: {#S2.SS4.p1 .ltx_para}
Recent progress in retrieval has mostly focused on large-data
evaluation, where many tens of thousands of annotated training queries
are associated with the test domain, as in MS MARCO or Natural Questions
([Kwiatkowski et al. 2019](#bib.bib34){.ltx_ref}). In these benchmarks,
queries tend to reflect high-popularity topics like movies and athletes
in Wikipedia. In practice, user-facing IR and QA applications often
pertain to domain-specific corpora, for which little to no training data
is available and whose topics are under-represented in large public
collections.
:::

::: {#S2.SS4.p2 .ltx_para}
This out-of-domain regime has received recent attention with the
BEIR [Thakur et al. 2021](#bib.bib58){.ltx_ref} benchmark. BEIR combines
several existing datasets into a heterogeneous suite for "zero-shot IR"
tasks, spanning bio-medical, financial, and scientific domains. While
the BEIR datasets provide a useful testbed, many capture broad semantic
relatedness tasks---like citations, counter arguments, or duplicate
questions--instead of natural search tasks, or else they focus on
high-popularity entities like those in Wikipedia. In §[[4]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S4 "4 LoTTE: Long-Tail, Cross-Domain Retrieval Evaluation ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref},
we introduce LoTTE, a new dataset for out-of-domain retrieval,
exhibiting natural search queries over long-tail topics.
:::

<figure id="S2.F1" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="2112.01488v3/colbert.png" id="S2.F1.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="405"
height="243" alt="Refer to caption" />

</figure>
:::::
:::::::::::::::

:::::::::::::::::::::::: {#S3 .section .ltx_section}
## [3 ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_section}ColBERTv2 {#colbertv2 .ltx_title .ltx_title_section}

::: {#S3.p1 .ltx_para}
We now introduce ColBERTv2, which improves the quality of multi-vector
retrieval models (§[[3.2]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Supervision ‣ 3 ColBERTv2 ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref})
while reducing their space footprint (§[[3.3]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S3.SS3 "3.3 Representation ‣ 3 ColBERTv2 ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref}).
:::

:::: {#S3.SS1 .section .ltx_subsection}
### [3.1 ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_subsection}Modeling {#modeling .ltx_title .ltx_title_subsection}

::: {#S3.SS1.p1 .ltx_para}
ColBERTv2 adopts the late interaction architecture of ColBERT, depicted
in Figure [[1]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S2.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ 2.4 Out-of-Domain Evaluation in IR ‣ 2 Background & Related Work ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref}.
Queries and passages are independently encoded with BERT [Devlin et al.
2019](#bib.bib12){.ltx_ref}, and the output embeddings encoding each
token are projected to a lower dimension. During offline indexing, every
passage $d$ in the corpus is encoded into a set of vectors, and these
vectors are stored. At search time, the query $q$ is encoded into a
multi-vector representation, and its similarity to a passage $d$ is
computed as the summation of query-side "MaxSim" operations, namely, the
largest cosine similarity between each query token embedding and all
passage token embeddings:

  -- -------------------------------------------------------------- -- ----------------------------------------------------
     $$S_{q,d}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\max_{j=1}^{M}Q_{i}\cdot D_{j}^{T}$$      [(1)]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_equation .ltx_align_right}
  -- -------------------------------------------------------------- -- ----------------------------------------------------

where $Q$ is an matrix encoding the query with $N$ vectors and $D$
encodes the passage with $M$ vectors. The intuition of this architecture
is to align each query token with the most contextually relevant passage
token, quantify these matches, and combine the partial scores across the
query. We refer to [Khattab and Zaharia 2020](#bib.bib33){.ltx_ref} for
a more detailed treatment of late interaction.
:::
::::

:::::::: {#S3.SS2 .section .ltx_subsection}
### [3.2 ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_subsection}Supervision {#supervision .ltx_title .ltx_title_subsection}

::: {#S3.SS2.p1 .ltx_para}
Training a neural retriever typically requires [positive]{#S3.SS2.p1.1.1
.ltx_text .ltx_font_italic} and [negative]{#S3.SS2.p1.1.2 .ltx_text
.ltx_font_italic} passages for each query in the training set. [Khattab
and Zaharia 2020](#bib.bib33){.ltx_ref} train ColBERT using the official
$\langle$q, d^+^, d${}^{-}\rangle$ triples of MS MARCO. For each query,
a positive $d^{+}$ is human-annotated, and each negative $d^{-}$ is
sampled from unannotated BM25-retrieved passages.
:::

::: {#S3.SS2.p2 .ltx_para}
Subsequent work has identified several weaknesses in this standard
supervision approach (see §[[2.3]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S2.SS3 "2.3 Improving the Quality of Single-Vector Representations ‣ 2 Background & Related Work ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref}).
Our goal is to adopt a simple, uniform supervision scheme that selects
challenging negatives and avoids rewarding false positives or penalizing
false negatives. To this end, we start with a ColBERT model trained with
triples as in [Khattab et al. 2021b](#bib.bib32){.ltx_ref}, using this
to index the training passages with ColBERTv2 compression.
:::

::: {#S3.SS2.p3 .ltx_para}
For each training query, we retrieve the top-$k$ passages. We feed each
of those query--passage pairs into a cross-encoder reranker. We use a
22M-parameter MiniLM [Wang et al. 2020](#bib.bib63){.ltx_ref}
cross-encoder trained with distillation by [Thakur et al.
2021](#bib.bib58){.ltx_ref}.[^2^[[^2^ [2]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_note}
[https://huggingface.co/cross-encoder/ms-marco-MiniLM-L-6-v2](https://huggingface.co/cross-encoder/ms-marco-MiniLM-L-6-v2){.ltx_ref
.ltx_url
.ltx_font_typewriter}]{.ltx_note_content}]{.ltx_note_outer}]{#footnote2
.ltx_note .ltx_role_footnote} This small model has been shown to exhibit
very strong performance while being relatively efficient for inference,
making it suitable for distillation.
:::

::: {#S3.SS2.p4 .ltx_para}
We then collect $w$-way tuples consisting of a query, a highly-ranked
passage (or labeled positive), and one or more lower-ranked passages. In
this work, we use $w=64$ passages per example. Like RocketQAv2 [Ren
et al. 2021b](#bib.bib56){.ltx_ref}, we use a KL-Divergence loss to
distill the cross-encoder's scores into the ColBERT architecture. We use
KL-Divergence as ColBERT produces scores (i.e., the sum of cosine
similarities) with a restricted scale, which may not align directly with
the output scores of the cross-encoder. We also employ in-batch
negatives per GPU, where a cross-entropy loss is applied to the positive
score of each query against all passages corresponding to other queries
in the same batch. We repeat this procedure once to refresh the index
and thus the sampled negatives.
:::

::: {#S3.SS2.p5 .ltx_para}
Denoised training with hard negatives has been positioned in recent work
as ways to bridge the gap between single-vector and interaction-based
models, including late interaction architectures like ColBERT. Our
results in §[[5]{.ltx_text .ltx_ref_tag}](#S5 "5 Evaluation ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref}
reveal that such supervision can improve multi-vector models
dramatically, resulting in state-of-the-art retrieval quality.
:::
::::::::

:::::: {#S3.SS3 .section .ltx_subsection}
### [3.3 ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_subsection}Representation {#representation .ltx_title .ltx_title_subsection}

::: {#S3.SS3.p1 .ltx_para}
We hypothesize that the ColBERT vectors cluster into regions that
capture highly-specific token semantics. We test this hypothesis in
Appendix [[A]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#A1 "Appendix A Analysis of ColBERT’s Semantic Space ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref},
where evidence suggests that vectors corresponding to each sense of a
word cluster closely, with only minor variation due to context. We
exploit this regularity with a [residual]{#S3.SS3.p1.1.1 .ltx_text
.ltx_font_italic} representation that dramatically reduces the space
footprint of late interaction models, completely
[off-the-shelf]{#S3.SS3.p1.1.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_italic} without
architectural or training changes. Given a set of centroids $C$,
ColBERTv2 encodes each vector $v$ as the index of its closest centroid
$C_{t}$ and a [quantized]{#S3.SS3.p1.1.3 .ltx_text .ltx_font_italic}
vector $\tilde{r}$ that approximates the residual $r=v-C_{t}$. At search
time, we use the centroid index $t$ and residual $\tilde{r}$ recover an
approximate $\tilde{v}=C_{t}+\tilde{r}$.
:::

::: {#S3.SS3.p2 .ltx_para}
To encode $\tilde{r}$, we quantize every dimension of $r$ into one or
two bits. In principle, our $b$-bit encoding of $n$-dimensional vectors
needs $\lceil\log{|C|}\rceil+bn$ bits per vector. In practice, with
$n=128$, we use four bytes to capture up to $2^{32}$ centroids and 16 or
32 bytes (for $b=1$ or $b=2$) to encode the residual. This total of 20
or 36 bytes per vector contrasts with ColBERT's use of 256-byte vector
encodings at 16-bit precision. While many alternatives can be explored
for compression, we find that this simple encoding largely preserves
model quality, while considerably lowering storage costs against typical
32- or 16-bit precision used by existing late interaction systems.
:::

::: {#S3.SS3.p3 .ltx_para}
This centroid-based encoding can be considered a natural extension of
product quantization to [multi-vector]{#S3.SS3.p3.1.1 .ltx_text
.ltx_font_italic} representations. Product quantization [Gray
1984](#bib.bib19){.ltx_ref}; [Jegou et al. 2010](#bib.bib25){.ltx_ref}
compresses a single vector by splitting it into small sub-vectors and
encoding each of them using an ID within a codebook. In our approach,
each representation is already a matrix that is naturally divided into a
number of small vectors (one per token). We encode each vector using its
nearest centroid plus a residual. Refer to Appendix [[B]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#A2 "Appendix B Impact of Compression ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref}
for tests of the impact of compression on retrieval quality and a
comparison with a baseline compression method for ColBERT akin to
BPR [Yamada et al. 2021b](#bib.bib69){.ltx_ref}.
:::

::::::

::::::: {#S3.SS4 .section .ltx_subsection}
### [3.4 ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_subsection}Indexing {#indexing .ltx_title .ltx_title_subsection}

::: {#S3.SS4.p1 .ltx_para}
Given a corpus of passages, the indexing stage precomputes all passage
embeddings and organizes their representations to support fast
nearest-neighbor search. ColBERTv2 divides indexing into three stages,
described below.
:::

::: {#S3.SS4.p2 .ltx_para}
[Centroid Selection.]{#S3.SS4.p2.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold} In the
first stage, ColBERTv2 selects a set of cluster centroids $C$. These are
embeddings that ColBERTv2 uses to support residual encoding
(§[[3.3]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S3.SS3 "3.3 Representation ‣ 3 ColBERTv2 ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref})
and also for nearest-neighbor search (§[[3.5]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S3.SS5 "3.5 Retrieval ‣ 3 ColBERTv2 ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref}).
Standardly, we find that setting $|C|$ proportionally to the square root
of $n_{\text{embeddings}}$ in the corpus works well
empirically.[^3^[[^3^ [3]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_note} We round down to the
nearest power of two larger than $16\times\sqrt{n_{\text{embeddings}}}$,
inspired by FAISS [Johnson et al.
2019](#bib.bib27){.ltx_ref}.]{.ltx_note_content}]{.ltx_note_outer}]{#footnote3
.ltx_note .ltx_role_footnote} [Khattab and Zaharia
2020](#bib.bib33){.ltx_ref} only clustered the vectors after computing
the representations of all passages, but doing so requires storing them
uncompressed. To reduce memory consumption, we apply $k$-means
clustering to the embeddings produced by invoking our BERT encoder over
only a sample of all passages, proportional to the square root of the
collection size, an approach we found to perform well in practice.
:::

::: {#S3.SS4.p3 .ltx_para}
[Passage Encoding.]{#S3.SS4.p3.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold} Having
selected the centroids, we encode every passage in the corpus. This
entails invoking the BERT encoder and compressing the output embeddings
as described in §[[3.3]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S3.SS3 "3.3 Representation ‣ 3 ColBERTv2 ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref},
assigning each embedding to the nearest centroid and computing a
quantized residual. Once a chunk of passages is encoded, the
[compressed]{#S3.SS4.p3.1.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_italic} representations
are saved to disk.
:::

::: {#S3.SS4.p4 .ltx_para}
[Index Inversion.]{#S3.SS4.p4.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold} To support
fast nearest-neighbor search, we group the embedding IDs that correspond
to each centroid together, and save this [inverted list]{#S3.SS4.p4.1.2
.ltx_text .ltx_font_italic} to disk. At search time, this allows us to
quickly find token-level embeddings similar to those in a query.
:::
:::::::

::::: {#S3.SS5 .section .ltx_subsection}
### [3.5 ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_subsection}Retrieval {#retrieval .ltx_title .ltx_title_subsection}

::: {#S3.SS5.p1 .ltx_para}
Given a query representation $Q$, retrieval starts with candidate
generation. For every vector $Q_{i}$ in the query, the nearest
$n_{\text{probe}}\geq 1$ centroids are found. Using the inverted list,
ColBERTv2 identifies the passage embeddings close to these centroids,
decompresses them, and computes their cosine similarity with every query
vector. The scores are then grouped by passage ID for each query vector,
and scores corresponding to the same passage are $\max$-reduced. This
allows ColBERTv2 to conduct an approximate "MaxSim" operation per query
vector. This computes a lower-bound on the true MaxSim
(§[[3.1]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S3.SS1 "3.1 Modeling ‣ 3 ColBERTv2 ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref})
using the embeddings identified via the inverted list, which resembles
the approximation explored for scoring by [Macdonald and Tonellotto
2021](#bib.bib45){.ltx_ref} but is applied for candidate generation.
:::

::: {#S3.SS5.p2 .ltx_para}
These lower bounds are summed across the query tokens, and the
top-scoring $n_{\text{candidate}}$ candidate passages based on these
approximate scores are selected for ranking, which loads the complete
set of embeddings of each passage, and conducts the same scoring
function using all embeddings per document following
Equation [[1]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S3.E1 "In 3.1 Modeling ‣ 3 ColBERTv2 ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref}.
The result passages are then sorted by score and returned.
:::
:::::
::::::::::::::::::::::::

:::::::::: {#S4 .section .ltx_section}
## [4 ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_section}LoTTE: Long-Tail, Cross-Domain Retrieval Evaluation {#lotte-long-tail-cross-domain-retrieval-evaluation .ltx_title .ltx_title_section}

::: {#S4.p1 .ltx_para}
We introduce [LoTTE]{#S4.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold} (pronounced
latte), a new dataset for [Lo]{#S4.p1.1.2 .ltx_text
.ltx_font_bold}ng-[T]{#S4.p1.1.3 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}ail
[T]{#S4.p1.1.4 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}opic-stratified [E]{#S4.p1.1.5
.ltx_text .ltx_font_bold}valuation for IR. To complement the
out-of-domain tests of BEIR [Thakur et al. 2021](#bib.bib58){.ltx_ref},
as motivated in §[[2.4]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S2.SS4 "2.4 Out-of-Domain Evaluation in IR ‣ 2 Background & Related Work ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref},
LoTTE focuses on [natural user queries]{#S4.p1.1.6 .ltx_text
.ltx_font_italic} that pertain to [long-tail topics]{#S4.p1.1.7
.ltx_text .ltx_font_italic}, ones that might not be covered by an
entity-centric knowledge base like Wikipedia. LoTTE consists of 12 test
sets, each with 500--2000 queries and 100k--2M passages.
:::

::: {#S4.p2 .ltx_para}
The test sets are explicitly divided by topic, and each test set is
accompanied by a validation set of [related but disjoint]{#S4.p2.1.1
.ltx_text .ltx_font_italic} queries [and]{#S4.p2.1.2 .ltx_text
.ltx_font_italic} passages. We elect to make the passage texts disjoint
to encourage more realistic out-of-domain transfer tests, allowing for
minimal development on related but distinct topics. The test (and dev)
sets include a "pooled" setting. In the pooled setting, the passages and
queries are aggregated across all test (or dev) topics to evaluate
out-of-domain retrieval across a larger and more diverse corpus.
:::

::: {#S4.p3 .ltx_para}
Table [[1]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S3.T1 "Table 1 ‣ 3.3 Representation ‣ 3 ColBERTv2 ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref}
outlines the composition of LoTTE. We derive the topics and passage
corpora from the [answer posts]{#S4.p3.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_italic}
across various StackExchange forums. StackExchange is a set of
question-and-answer communities that target individual topics (e.g.,
"physics" or "bicycling"). We gather forums from five overarching
domains: writing, recreation, science, technology, and lifestyle. To
evaluate retrievers, we collect [Search]{#S4.p3.1.2 .ltx_text
.ltx_font_italic} and [Forum]{#S4.p3.1.3 .ltx_text .ltx_font_italic}
queries, each of which is associated with one or more target answer
posts in its corpus. Example queries, and short snippets from posts that
answer them in the corpora, are shown in Table [[2]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S4.T2 "Table 2 ‣ 4 LoTTE: Long-Tail, Cross-Domain Retrieval Evaluation ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref}.
:::

::: {#S4.p4 .ltx_para}
[Search Queries.]{#S4.p4.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold} We collect search
queries from GooAQ [Khashabi et al. 2021](#bib.bib30){.ltx_ref}, a
recent dataset of Google search-autocomplete queries and their answer
boxes, which we filter for queries whose answers link to a specific
StackExchange post. As [Khashabi et al. 2021](#bib.bib30){.ltx_ref}
hypothesize, Google Search likely maps these natural queries to their
answers by relying on a wide variety of signals for relevance, including
expert annotations, user clicks, and hyperlinks as well as specialized
QA components for various question types [with access to the post title
and question body]{#S4.p4.1.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_italic}. Using those
annotations as ground truth, we evaluate the models on their capacity
for retrieval using [only]{#S4.p4.1.3 .ltx_text .ltx_font_italic} free
text of the answer posts (i.e., no hyperlinks or user clicks, question
title or body, etc.), posing a significant challenge for IR and NLP
systems trained only on public datasets.
:::

spider?</span>    <span id="S4.T3.2.2.1.1.1.19"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Q:</span> <span id="S4.T3.2.2.1.1.1.20"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">What does 1x1 convolution mean in a
neural network?</span></span> </span></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table 3:
</span>Comparison of a random sample of search queries (top) vs. forum
queries (bottom).</figcaption>
</figure>

::: {#S4.p5 .ltx_para}
[Forum Queries.]{#S4.p5.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold} We collect the
forum queries by extracting post titles from the StackExchange
communities to use as queries and collect their corresponding answer
posts as targets. We select questions in order of their popularity and
sample questions according to the proportional contribution of
individual communities within each topic. These queries tend to have a
wider variety than the "search" queries, while the search queries may
exhibit more natural patterns. Table [[3]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S4.T3 "Table 3 ‣ 4 LoTTE: Long-Tail, Cross-Domain Retrieval Evaluation ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref}
compares a random samples of search and forum queries. It can be seen
that search queries tend to be brief, knowledge-based questions with
direct answers, whereas forum queries tend to reflect more open-ended
questions. Both query sets target topics that exceed the scope of a
general-purpose knowledge repository such as Wikipedia.
:::

::: {#S4.p6 .ltx_para}
For search as well as forum queries, the resulting evaluation set
consists of a query and a target set of StackExchange answer posts (in
particular, the answer posts from the target StackExchange page).
Similar to evaluation in the Open-QA literature [Karpukhin et al.
2020](#bib.bib29){.ltx_ref}; [Khattab et al.
2021b](#bib.bib32){.ltx_ref}, we evaluate retrieval quality by computing
the [success@5]{#S4.p6.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_typewriter} (S@5) metric.
Specifically, we award a point to the system for each query where it
finds an accepted or upvoted (score $\geq$ 1) answer from the target
page in the top-5 hits.
:::

::: {#S4.p7 .ltx_para}
Appendix [[D]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#A4 "Appendix D LoTTE ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref}
reports on the breakdown of constituent communities per topic, the
construction procedure of LoTTE as well as licensing considerations, and
relevant statistics. Figures [[5]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#A4.F5 "Figure 5 ‣ Appendix D LoTTE ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref}
and [[6]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#A4.F6 "Figure 6 ‣ Appendix D LoTTE ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref}
quantitatively compare the search and forum queries.
:::
::::::::::

:::::::::::::::::::: {#S5 .section .ltx_section}
## [5 ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_section}Evaluation {#evaluation .ltx_title .ltx_title_section}

::: {#S5.p1 .ltx_para}
We now evaluate ColBERTv2 on passage retrieval tasks, testing its
quality within the training domain (§[[5.1]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S5.SS1 "5.1 In-Domain Retrieval Quality ‣ 5 Evaluation ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref})
as well as outside the training domain in zero-shot settings
(§[[5.2]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S5.SS2 "5.2 Out-of-Domain Retrieval Quality ‣ 5 Evaluation ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref}).
Unless otherwise stated, we compress ColBERTv2 embeddings to $b=2$ bits
per dimension in our evaluation.
:::

:::::: {#S5.SS1 .section .ltx_subsection}
### [5.1 ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_subsection}In-Domain Retrieval Quality {#in-domain-retrieval-quality .ltx_title .ltx_title_subsection}

::: {#S5.SS1.p1 .ltx_para}
Similar to related work, we train for IR tasks on MS MARCO Passage
Ranking [Nguyen et al. 2016](#bib.bib49){.ltx_ref}. Within the training
domain, our development-set results are shown in Table [[4]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S5.T4 "Table 4 ‣ 5 Evaluation ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref},
comparing ColBERTv2 with vanilla ColBERT as well as state-of-the-art
single-vector systems.
:::

::: {#S5.SS1.p2 .ltx_para}
While ColBERT outperforms single-vector systems like RepBERT, ANCE, and
even TAS-B, improvements in supervision such as distillation from
cross-encoders enable systems like SPLADEv2, PAIR, and RocketQAv2 to
achieve higher quality than vanilla ColBERT. These supervision gains
challenge the value of fine-grained late interaction, and it is not
inherently clear whether the stronger inductive biases of ColBERT-like
models permit it to accept similar gains under distillation, especially
when using compressed representations. Despite this, we find that with
denoised supervision and residual compression, ColBERTv2 achieves the
highest quality across all systems. As we discuss in §[[5.3]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S5.SS3 "5.3 Efficiency ‣ 5 Evaluation ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref},
it exhibits space footprint competitive with these single-vector models
and much lower than vanilla ColBERT.
:::

::: {#S5.SS1.p3 .ltx_para}
Besides the official dev set, we evaluated ColBERTv2, SPLADEv2, and
RocketQAv2 on the "Local Eval" test set described by [Khattab and
Zaharia 2020](#bib.bib33){.ltx_ref} for MS MARCO, which consists of 5000
queries disjoint with the training and the official dev sets. These
queries are obtained from labeled 50k queries that are provided in the
official MS MARCO Passage Ranking task as additional validation
data.[^4^[[^4^ [4]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_note} These are sampled from delta
between [qrels.dev.tsv]{#footnote4.2 .ltx_text .ltx_font_typewriter} and
[qrels.dev.small.tsv]{#footnote4.3 .ltx_text .ltx_font_typewriter} on
[https://microsoft.github.io/msmarco/Datasets](https://microsoft.github.io/msmarco/Datasets){.ltx_ref
.ltx_url .ltx_font_typewriter}. We refer to [Khattab and Zaharia
2020](#bib.bib33){.ltx_ref} for details. All our query IDs will be made
public to aid
reproducibility.]{.ltx_note_content}]{.ltx_note_outer}]{#footnote4
.ltx_note .ltx_role_footnote} On this test set, ColBERTv2 obtains 40.8%
MRR@10, considerably outperforming the baselines, including RocketQAv2
which makes use of document titles in addition to the passage text
unlike the other systems.
:::
::::::

:::::::::: {#S5.SS2 .section .ltx_subsection}
### [5.2 ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_subsection}Out-of-Domain Retrieval Quality {#out-of-domain-retrieval-quality .ltx_title .ltx_title_subsection}

::: {#S5.SS2.p1 .ltx_para}
Next, we evaluate ColBERTv2 outside the training domain using
BEIR [Thakur et al. 2021](#bib.bib58){.ltx_ref}, Wikipedia Open QA
retrieval as in [Khattab et al. 2021b](#bib.bib32){.ltx_ref}, and LoTTE.
We compare against a wide range of recent and state-of-the-art retrieval
systems from the literature.
:::

::: {#S5.SS2.p2 .ltx_para}
[BEIR.]{#S5.SS2.p2.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold} We start with BEIR,
reporting the quality of models that do not incorporate distillation
from cross-encoders, namely, ColBERT [Khattab and Zaharia
2020](#bib.bib33){.ltx_ref}, DPR-MARCO [Xin et al.
2021](#bib.bib66){.ltx_ref}, ANCE [Xiong et al.
2020](#bib.bib67){.ltx_ref}, and MoDIR [Xin et al.
2021](#bib.bib66){.ltx_ref}, as well as models that do utilize
distillation, namely, TAS-B [Hofstätter et al.
2021](#bib.bib22){.ltx_ref}, SPLADEv2 [Formal et al.
2021a](#bib.bib14){.ltx_ref}, and also RocketQAv2, which we test
ourselves using the official checkpoint trained on MS MARCO. We divide
the table into ''search'' (i.e., natural queries and questions) and
''semantic relatednes'' (e.g., citation-relatedness and claim
verification) tasks to reflect the nature of queries in each
dataset.[^5^[[^5^ [5]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_note} Following [Formal et al.
2021a](#bib.bib14){.ltx_ref}, we conduct our evaluationg using the
publicly-available datasets in BEIR. Refer to §[[E]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#A5 "Appendix E Datasets in BEIR ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref}
for details.]{.ltx_note_content}]{.ltx_note_outer}]{#footnote5 .ltx_note
.ltx_role_footnote}
:::

::: {#S5.SS2.p3 .ltx_para}
Table [[5(a)]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S5.T5.st1 "In Table 5 ‣ 5 Evaluation ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref}
reports results with the official nDCG@10 metric. Among the models
without distillation, we see that the vanilla ColBERT model outperforms
the single-vector systems DPR, ANCE, and MoDIR across all but three
tasks. ColBERT often outpaces all three systems by large margins and, in
fact, outperforms the TAS-B model, which utilizes distillation, on most
datasets. Shifting our attention to models with distillation, we see a
similar pattern: while distillation-based models are generally stronger
than their vanilla counterparts, the models that decompose scoring into
term-level interactions, ColBERTv2 and SPLADEv2, are almost always the
strongest.
:::

::: {#S5.SS2.p4 .ltx_para}
Looking more closely into the comparison between SPLADEv2 and ColBERTv2,
we see that ColBERTv2 has an advantage on six benchmarks and ties
SPLADEv2 on two, with the largest improvements attained on NQ,
TREC-COVID, and FiQA-2018, all of which feature natural search queries.
On the other hand, SPLADEv2 has the lead on five benchmarks, displaying
the largest gains on Climate-FEVER (C-FEVER) and HotPotQA. In C-FEVER,
the input queries are sentences making climate-related claims and, as a
result, do not reflect the typical characteristics of search queries. In
HotPotQA, queries are written by crowdworkers who have access to the
target passages. This is known to lead to artificial lexical bias [Lee
et al. 2019](#bib.bib37){.ltx_ref}, where crowdworkers copy terms from
the passages into their questions as in the Open-SQuAD benchmark.
:::

::: {#S5.SS2.p5 .ltx_para}
[Wikipedia Open QA.]{#S5.SS2.p5.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold} As a
further test of out-of-domain generalization, we evaluate the MS
MARCO-trained ColBERTv2, SPLADEv2, and vanilla ColBERT on retrieval for
open-domain question answering, similar to the out-of-domain setting of
[Khattab et al. 2021b](#bib.bib32){.ltx_ref}. We report Success@5
(sometimes referred to as Recall@5), which is the percentage of
questions whose short answer string overlaps with one or more of the
top-5 passages. For the queries, we use the development set questions of
the open-domain versions [Lee et al. 2019](#bib.bib37){.ltx_ref};
[Karpukhin et al. 2020](#bib.bib29){.ltx_ref} of Natural Questions (NQ;
[Kwiatkowski et al. 2019](#bib.bib34){.ltx_ref}), TriviaQA (TQ; [Joshi
et al. 2017](#bib.bib28){.ltx_ref}), and SQuAD [Rajpurkar et al.
2016](#bib.bib54){.ltx_ref} datasets in Table [[5(b)]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S5.T5.st2 "In Table 5 ‣ 5 Evaluation ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref}.
As a baseline, we include the BM25 [Robertson et al.
1995](#bib.bib57){.ltx_ref} results using the Anserini [Yang et al.
2018a](#bib.bib70){.ltx_ref} toolkit. We observe that ColBERTv2
outperforms BM25, vanilla ColBERT, and SPLADEv2 across the three query
sets, with improvements of up to 4.6 points over SPLADEv2.
:::

::: {#S5.SS2.p6 .ltx_para}
[LoTTE.]{#S5.SS2.p6.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_bold} Next, we analyze
performance on the LoTTE test benchmark, which focuses on natural
queries over long-tail topics and exhibits a different annotation
pattern to the datasets in the previous OOD evaluations. In particular,
LoTTE uses automatic Google rankings (for the "search" queries) and
organic StackExchange question--answer pairs (for "forum" queries),
complimenting the pooling-based annotation of datasets like TREC-COVID
(in BEIR) and the answer overlap metrics of Open-QA retrieval. We report
Success@5 for each corpus on both search queries and forum queries.
:::

::: {#S5.SS2.p7 .ltx_para}
Overall, we see that ANCE and vanilla ColBERT outperform BM25 on all
topics, and that the three methods using distillation are generally the
strongest. Similar to the Wikipedia-OpenQA results, we find that
ColBERTv2 outperforms the baselines across all topics for both query
types, improving upon SPLADEv2 and RocketQAv2 by up to 3.7 and 8.1
points, respectively. Considering the baselines, we observe that while
RocketQAv2 tends to have a slight advantage over SPLADEv2 on the
"search" queries, SPLADEv2 is considerably more effective on the "forum"
tests. We hypothesize that the search queries, obtained from Google
(through GooAQ) are more similar to MS MARCO than the forum queries and,
as a result, the latter stresses generalization more heavily, rewarding
term-decomposed models like SPLADEv2 and ColBERTv2.
:::
::::::::::

:::::: {#S5.SS3 .section .ltx_subsection}
### [5.3 ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_subsection}Efficiency {#efficiency .ltx_title .ltx_title_subsection}

::: {#S5.SS3.p1 .ltx_para}
ColBERTv2's residual compression approach significantly reduces index
sizes compared to vanilla ColBERT. Whereas ColBERT requires 154 GiB to
store the index for MS MARCO, ColBERTv2 only requires 16 GiB or 25 GiB
when compressing embeddings to 1 or 2 bit(s) per dimension,
respectively, resulting in compression ratios of 6--10$\times$. This
storage figure includes 4.5 GiB for storing the inverted list.
:::

::: {#S5.SS3.p2 .ltx_para}
This matches the storage for a typical single-vector model on MS MARCO,
with 4-byte lossless floating-point storage for one 768-dimensional
vector for each of the 9M passages amounting to a little over 25 GiBs.
In practice, the storage for a single-vector model could be even larger
when using a nearest-neighbor index like HNSW for fast search.
Conversely, single-vector representations could be themselves compressed
very aggressively [Zhan et al. 2021a](#bib.bib72){.ltx_ref}; [Zhan
et al. 2022](#bib.bib74){.ltx_ref}, though often exacerbating the loss
in quality relative to late interaction methods like ColBERTv2.
:::

::: {#S5.SS3.p3 .ltx_para}
We discuss the impact of our compression method on search quality in
Appendix [[B]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#A2 "Appendix B Impact of Compression ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref}
and present query latency results on the order of 50--250 milliseconds
per query in Appendix [[C]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#A3 "Appendix C Retrieval Latency ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref}.
:::
::::::
::::::::::::::::::::

:::: {#S6 .section .ltx_section}
## [6 ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_section}Conclusion {#conclusion .ltx_title .ltx_title_section}

::: {#S6.p1 .ltx_para}
We introduced ColBERTv2, a retriever that advances the quality and space
efficiency of multi-vector representations. We hypothesized that cluster
centroids capture context-aware semantics of the token-level
representations and proposed a residual representation that leverages
these patterns to dramatically reduce the footprint of multi-vector
systems [off-the-shelf]{#S6.p1.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_italic}. We then
explored improved supervision for multi-vector retrieval and found that
their quality improves considerably upon distillation from a
cross-encoder system. The proposed ColBERTv2 considerably outperforms
existing retrievers in within-domain and out-of-domain evaluations,
which we conducted extensively across 28 datasets, establishing
state-of-the-art quality while exhibiting competitive space footprint.
:::
::::

:::: {#Sx1 .section .ltx_section}
## Acknowledgements {#acknowledgements .ltx_title .ltx_title_section}

::::

::::: {#Sx2 .section .ltx_section}
## Broader Impact & Ethical Considerations {#broader-impact-ethical-considerations .ltx_title .ltx_title_section}

::: {#Sx2.p1 .ltx_para}
This work is primarily an effort toward retrieval models that generalize
better while performing reasonably efficiently in terms of space
consumption. Strong out-of-the-box generalization to small
domain-specific applications can serve many users in practice,
particularly where training data is not available. Moreover, retrieval
holds significant promise for many downstream NLP tasks, as it can help
make language models smaller and thus more efficient (i.e., by
decoupling knowledge from computation), more transparent (i.e., by
allowing users to check the sources the model relied on when making a
claim or prediction), and easier to update (i.e., by allowing developers
to replace or add documents to the corpus without retraining the model)
[Guu et al. 2020](#bib.bib20){.ltx_ref}; [Borgeaud et al.
2021](#bib.bib6){.ltx_ref}; [Khattab et al.
2021a](#bib.bib31){.ltx_ref}. Nonetheless, such work poses risks in
terms of misuse, particularly toward misinformation, as retrieval can
surface results that are relevant yet inaccurate, depending on the
contents of a corpus. Moreover, generalization from training on a
large-scale dataset can propagate the biases of that dataset well beyond
its typical reach to new domains and applications.
:::

::: {#Sx2.p2 .ltx_para}
While our contributions have made ColBERT's late interaction more
efficient at storage costs, large-scale distillation with hard negatives
increases system complexity and accordingly increases training cost,
when compared with the straightforward training paradigm of the original
ColBERT model. While ColBERTv2 is efficient in terms of latency and
storage at inference time, we suspect that under extreme resource
constraints, simpler model designs like SPLADEv2 or RocketQAv2 could
lend themselves to easier-to-optimize environments. We leave low-level
systems optimizations of all systems to future work. Another worthwhile
dimension for future exploration of tradeoffs is re-ranking
architectures over various systems with cross-encoders, which are known
to be expensive yet precise due to their highly expressive capacity.
:::
:::::

::::::: {#Sx3 .section .ltx_section}
## Research Limitations {#research-limitations .ltx_title .ltx_title_section}

::: {#Sx3.p1 .ltx_para}
While we evaluate ColBERTv2 on a wide range of tests, all of our
benchmarks are in English and, in line with related work, our
out-of-domain tests evaluate models that are trained on MS MARCO. We
expect our approach to work effectively for other languages and when all
models are trained using other, smaller training set (e.g.,
NaturalQuestions), but we leave such tests to future work.
:::

::: {#Sx3.p2 .ltx_para}
We have observed consistent gains for ColBERTv2 against existing
state-of-the-art systems across many diverse settings. Despite this,
almost all IR datasets contain false negatives (i.e., relevant but
unlabeled passages) and thus some caution is needed in interpreting any
individual result. Nonetheless, we intentionally sought out benchmarks
with dissimilar annotation biases: for instance, TREC-COVID (in BEIR)
annotates the pool of documents retrieved by the systems submitted at
the time of the competition, LoTTE uses automatic Google rankings (for
"search" queries) and StackExchange question--answer pairs (for "forum"
queries), and the Open-QA tests rely on passage-answer overlap for
factoid questions. ColBERTv2 performed well in all of these settings. We
discuss other issues pertinent to LoTTE in Appendix §[[D]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#A4 "Appendix D LoTTE ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref}.
:::

::: {#Sx3.p3 .ltx_para}
We have compared with a wide range of strong baselines---including
sparse retrieval and single-vector models---and found reliable patterns
across tests. However, we caution that empirical trends can change as
innovations are introduced to each of these families of models and that
it can be difficult to ensure exact apple-to-apple comparisons across
families of models, since each of them calls for different sophisticated
tuning strategies. We thus primarily used results and models from the
rich recent literature on these problems, with models like RocketQAv2
and SPLADEv2.
:::

::: {#Sx3.p4 .ltx_para}
On the representational side, we focus on reducing the storage cost
using residual compression, achieving strong gains in reducing footprint
while largely preserving quality. Nonetheless, we have not exhausted the
space of more sophisticated optimizations possible, and we would expect
more sophisticated forms of residual compression and composing our
approach with dropping tokens [Zhou and Devlin
2021](#bib.bib77){.ltx_ref} to open up possibilities for further
reductions in space footprint.
:::
:::::::

::::::: {#A1 .section .ltx_appendix}
## [Appendix A ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_appendix}Analysis of ColBERT's Semantic Space {#appendix-a-analysis-of-colberts-semantic-space .ltx_title .ltx_title_appendix}

::: {#A1.p1 .ltx_para}
ColBERT [Khattab and Zaharia 2020](#bib.bib33){.ltx_ref} decomposes
representations and similarity computation at the token level. Because
of this compositional architecture, we hypothesize that ColBERT exhibits
a "lightweight" semantic space: without any special re-training, vectors
corresponding to each sense of a word would cluster very closely, with
only minor variation due to context.
:::

<figure id="A1.F2" class="ltx_figure">

class="ltx_figure ltx_figure_panel ltx_align_center">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">(a) </span>Number of
distinct tokens appearing in each cluster.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

</div>

class="ltx_figure ltx_figure_panel ltx_align_center">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">(b) </span>Number of

</div>
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 2:
</span>Empirical CDFs analyzing semantic properties of MS MARCO
token-level embeddings both encoded by ColBERT and randomly generated.
The embeddings are partitioned into <span
class="math inline">2<sup>18</sup></span> clusters and correspond to
roughly 27,000 distinct tokens.</figcaption>
</figure>

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table 6: </span>Examples
of clusters taken from all MS MARCO passages. We present the tokens that
appear most frequently in the selected clusters as well as additional
clusters the top tokens appear in.</figcaption>
</figure>

::: {#A1.p2 .ltx_para}
If this hypothesis is true, we would expect the embeddings corresponding
to each token in the vocabulary to localize in only a small number of
regions in the embedding space, corresponding to the contextual "senses"
of the token. To validate this hypothesis, we analyze the ColBERT
embeddings corresponding to the tokens in the MS MARCO Passage
Ranking [Nguyen et al. 2016](#bib.bib49){.ltx_ref} collection: we
perform $k$-means clustering on the nearly 600M
embeddings---corresponding to 27,000 unique tokens---into $k=2^{18}$
clusters. As a baseline, we repeat this clustering with random
embeddings but keep the true distribution of tokens.
Figure [[2]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#A1.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ Appendix A Analysis of ColBERT’s Semantic Space ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref}
presents empirical cumulative distribution function (eCDF) plots
representing the number of distinct non-stopword tokens appearing in
each cluster ([[2(a)]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#A1.F2.sf1 "In Figure 2 ‣ Appendix A Analysis of ColBERT’s Semantic Space ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref})
and the number of distinct clusters in which each token appears
([[2(b)]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#A1.F2.sf2 "In Figure 2 ‣ Appendix A Analysis of ColBERT’s Semantic Space ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref}).[^6^[[^6^
[6]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_note} We rank tokens by number of clusters they
appear in and designate the top-1% (under 300) as
stopwords.]{.ltx_note_content}]{.ltx_note_outer}]{#footnote6 .ltx_note
.ltx_role_footnote} Most tokens appear in a very small fraction of the
number of centroids: in particular, we see that roughly 90% of clusters
have $\leq$ 16 distinct tokens with the ColBERT embeddings, whereas less
than 50% of clusters have $\leq$ 16 distinct tokens with the random
embeddings. This suggests that the centroids effectively map the ColBERT
semantic space.
:::

::: {#A1.p3 .ltx_para}
Table [[6]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#A1.T6 "Table 6 ‣ Appendix A Analysis of ColBERT’s Semantic Space ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref}
presents examples to highlight the semantic space captured by the
centroids. The most frequently appearing tokens in cluster #917 relate
to photography; these include, for example, 'photos' and 'photographs'.
If we then examine the additional clusters in which these tokens appear,
we find that there is substantial semantic overlap between these new
clusters (e.g., Photos-Photo, Photo-Image-Picture) and cluster #917. We
observe a similar effect with tokens appearing in cluster #216932,
comprising tornado-related terms.
:::

::: {#A1.p4 .ltx_para}
This analysis indicates that cluster centroids can summarize the ColBERT
representations with high precision. In §[[3.3]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S3.SS3 "3.3 Representation ‣ 3 ColBERTv2 ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref},
we propose a residual compression mechanism that uses these centroids
along with minor refinements at the dimension level to efficiently
encode late-interaction vectors.
:::
:::::::

:::::: {#A2 .section .ltx_appendix}
## [Appendix B ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_appendix}Impact of Compression {#appendix-b-impact-of-compression .ltx_title .ltx_title_appendix}

::: {#A2.p1 .ltx_para}
Our residual compression approach (§[[3.3]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S3.SS3 "3.3 Representation ‣ 3 ColBERTv2 ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref})
preserves approximately the same quality as the uncompressed embeddings.
In particular, when applied to a vanilla ColBERT model on MS MARCO whose
MRR@10 is 36.2% and Recall@50 is 82.1%, the quality of the model with
2-bit compression is 36.2% MRR@10 and 82.3% Recall@50. With 1-bit
compression, the model achieves 35.5% MRR@10 and 81.6%
Recall@50.[^7^[[^7^ [7]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_note} We contrast this with an
early implementation of compression for ColBERT, which used binary
representations as in BPR [Yamada et al. 2021a](#bib.bib68){.ltx_ref}
without residual centroids, and achieves 34.8% (35.7%) MRR@10 and 80.5%
(81.8%) Recall@50 with 1-bit (2-bit) binarization. Like the original
ColBERT, this form of compression relied on a separate FAISS index for
candidate generation.]{.ltx_note_content}]{.ltx_note_outer}]{#footnote7
.ltx_note .ltx_role_footnote}
:::

<figure id="A2.F3" class="ltx_figure">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 3:
</span>Latency vs. retrieval quality with varying parameter
configurations for three datasets of different collection sizes. We
sweep a range of values for the number of centroids per vector (<span
id="A2.F3.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_markedasmath ltx_font_typewriter">probe</span>), the
number of bits used for residual compression, and the number of
candidates. Note that retrieval quality is measured in MRR@10 for MS
MARCO and Success@5 for LoTTE datasets. Results toward the bottom right
corner (higher quality, lower latency) are best.</figcaption>
</figure>

::: {#A2.p2 .ltx_para}
We also tested the residual compression approach on late-interaction
retrievers that conduct downstream tasks, namely, ColBERT-QA [Khattab
et al. 2021b](#bib.bib32){.ltx_ref} for the NaturalQuestions open-domain
QA task, and Baleen [Khattab et al. 2021a](#bib.bib31){.ltx_ref} for
multi-hop reasoning on HoVer for claim verification. On the NQ dev set,
ColBERT-QA's success@5 (success@20) dropped only marginally from 75.3%
(84.3%) to 74.3% (84.2%) and its downstream Open-QA answer exact match
dropped from 47.9% to 47.7%, when using 2-bit compression for retrieval
and using the same checkpoints of ColBERT-QA otherwise.
:::

::: {#A2.p3 .ltx_para}
Similarly, on the HoVer [Jiang et al. 2020](#bib.bib26){.ltx_ref} dev
set, Baleen's retrieval R@100 dropped from 92.2% to only 90.6% but its
sentence-level exact match remained roughly the same, going from 39.2%
to 39.4%. We hypothesize that the supervision methods applied in
ColBERTv2 (§[[3.2]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Supervision ‣ 3 ColBERTv2 ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref})
can also be applied to lift quality in downstream tasks by improving the
recall of retrieval for these tasks. We leave such exploration for
future work.
:::
::::::

::::::: {#A3 .section .ltx_appendix}
## [Appendix C ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_appendix}Retrieval Latency {#appendix-c-retrieval-latency .ltx_title .ltx_title_appendix}

::: {#A3.p1 .ltx_para}
Figure [[3]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#A2.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ Appendix B Impact of Compression ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref}
evaluates the latency of ColBERTv2 across three collections of varying
sizes, namely, MS MARCO, LoTTE Pooled (dev), and LoTTE Lifestyle (dev),
which contain approximately 9M passages, 2.4M answer posts, and 270k
answer posts, respectively. We average latency across three runs of the
MS MARCO dev set and the LoTTE "search" queries. Search is executed
using a Titan V GPU on a server with two Intel Xeon Gold 6132 CPUs, each
with 28 hardware execution contexts.
:::

::: {#A3.p2 .ltx_para}
The figure varies three settings of ColBERTv2. In particular, we
evaluate indexing with 1-bit and 2-bit encoding (§[[3.4]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S3.SS4 "3.4 Indexing ‣ 3 ColBERTv2 ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref})
and searching by probing the nearest 1, 2, or 4 centroids to each query
vector (§[[3.5]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S3.SS5 "3.5 Retrieval ‣ 3 ColBERTv2 ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref}).
When probing [probe]{#A3.p2.1.1 .ltx_text .ltx_font_typewriter}
centroids per vector, we score either $\texttt{probe}\times 2^{12}$ or
$\texttt{probe}\times 2^{14}$ candidates per query.[^8^[[^8^
[8]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_note} These settings are selected based on
preliminary exploration of these parameters, which indicated that
performance for larger [probe]{#footnote8.2 .ltx_text
.ltx_font_typewriter} values tends to require scoring a larger number of
candidates.]{.ltx_note_content}]{.ltx_note_outer}]{#footnote8 .ltx_note
.ltx_role_footnote}
:::

::: {#A3.p3 .ltx_para}
To begin with, we notice that the quality reported on the $x$-axis
varies only within a relatively narrow range. For instance, the axis
ranges from 38.50 through 39.75 for MS MARCO, and all but two of the
cheapest settings score above 39.00. Similarly, the $y$-axis varies
between approximately 50 milliseconds per query up to 250 milliseconds
(mostly under 150 milliseconds) using our relatively simple Python-based
implementation.
:::

::: {#A3.p4 .ltx_para}
Digging deeper, we see that the best quality in these metrics can be
achieved or approached closely with around 100 milliseconds of latency
across all three datasets, despite their various sizes and
characteristics, and that 2-bit indexing reliably outperforms 1-bit
indexing but the loss from more aggressive compression is small.
:::
:::::::

::::::::::::::::: {#A4 .section .ltx_appendix}
## [Appendix D ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_appendix}LoTTE {#appendix-d-lotte .ltx_title .ltx_title_appendix}

<figure id="A4.F4" class="ltx_figure">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 4: </span>LoTTE
words per passage</figcaption>
</figure>

<figure id="A4.F5" class="ltx_figure">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 5: </span>LoTTE
words per query</figcaption>
</figure>

<figure id="A4.F6" class="ltx_figure">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 6: </span>LoTTE
answers per query</figcaption>
</figure>

:::: {#A4.SS0.SSS0.Px1 .section .ltx_paragraph}
#### Domain coverage {#domain-coverage .ltx_title .ltx_title_paragraph}

::: {#A4.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1 .ltx_para}
Table [[9]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#A6.T9 "Table 9 ‣ Appendix F Implementation & Hyperparameters ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref}
presents the full distribution of communities in the LoTTE dev dataset.
The topics covered by LoTTE cover a wide range of linguistic phenomena
given the diversity in topics and communities represented. However,
since all posts are submitted by anonymous users we do not have
demographic information regarding the identify of the contributors. All
posts are written in English.
:::
::::

:::: {#A4.SS0.SSS0.Px2 .section .ltx_paragraph}
#### Passages {#passages .ltx_title .ltx_title_paragraph}

::: {#A4.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1 .ltx_para}
As mentioned in §[[4]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S4 "4 LoTTE: Long-Tail, Cross-Domain Retrieval Evaluation ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref},
we construct LoTTE collections by selecting passages from the
StackExchange archive with positive scores. We remove HTML tags from
passages and filter out empty passages. For each passage we record its
corresponding query and save the query-to-passage mapping to keep track
of the posted answers corresponding to each query.
:::
::::

:::: {#A4.SS0.SSS0.Px3 .section .ltx_paragraph}
#### Search queries {#search-queries .ltx_title .ltx_title_paragraph}

::: {#A4.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p1 .ltx_para}
We construct the list of LoTTE search queries by drawing from GooAQ
queries that appear in the StackExchange post archive. We first shuffle
the list of GooAQ queries so that in cases where multiple queries exist
for the same answer passage we randomly select the query to include in
LoTTE rather than always selecting the first appearing query. We verify
that every query has at least one corresponding answer passage.
:::
::::

:::: {#A4.SS0.SSS0.Px4 .section .ltx_paragraph}
#### Forum queries {#forum-queries .ltx_title .ltx_title_paragraph}

::: {#A4.SS0.SSS0.Px4.p1 .ltx_para}
For each LoTTE topic and its constituent communities we first compute
the fraction of the total queries attributed to each individual
community. We then use this distribution to construct a truncated query
set by selecting the highest ranked queries from each community as
determined by 1) the query scores and 2) the query view counts. We only
use queries which have an accepted answer. We ensure that each community
contributes at least 50 queries to the truncated set whenever possible.
We set the overall size of the truncated set to be 2000 queries, though
note that the total can exceed this due to rounding and/or the minimum
per-community query count. We remove all quotation marks and HTML tags.
:::
::::

:::: {#A4.SS0.SSS0.Px5 .section .ltx_paragraph}
#### Statistics {#statistics .ltx_title .ltx_title_paragraph}

::: {#A4.SS0.SSS0.Px5.p1 .ltx_para}
Figure [[4]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#A4.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ Appendix D LoTTE ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref}
plots the number of words per passage in each LoTTE dev corpus.
Figures [[5]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#A4.F5 "Figure 5 ‣ Appendix D LoTTE ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref}
and [[6]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#A4.F6 "Figure 6 ‣ Appendix D LoTTE ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref}
plot the number of words and number of corresponding answer passages
respectively per query, split across search and forum queries.
:::

:::: {#A4.SS0.SSS0.Px6 .section .ltx_paragraph}
#### Dev Results {#dev-results .ltx_title .ltx_title_paragraph}

::: {#A4.SS0.SSS0.Px6.p1 .ltx_para}
Table [[7]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#A4.T7 "Table 7 ‣ Statistics ‣ Appendix D LoTTE ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref}
presents out-of-domain evaluation results on the LoTTE dev queries.
Continuing the trend we observed in [[5]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#S5 "5 Evaluation ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref},
ColBERTv2 consistently outperforms all other models we tested.
:::
::::

:::: {#A4.SS0.SSS0.Px7 .section .ltx_paragraph}
#### Licensing and Anonymity {#licensing-and-anonymity .ltx_title .ltx_title_paragraph}

::: {#A4.SS0.SSS0.Px7.p1 .ltx_para}
The original StackExchange post archive is licensed under a Creative
Commons BY-SA 4.0 license [sta](#bib.bib1){.ltx_ref}. Personal data is
removed from the archive before being uploaded, though all posts are
public; when we release LoTTE publicly we will include URLs to the
original posts for proper attribution as required by the license. The
GooAQ dataset is licensed under an Apache license, version 2.0 [Khashabi
et al. 2021](#bib.bib30){.ltx_ref}. We will also release LoTTE with a CC
BY-SA 4.0 license. The search queries can be used for non-commercial
research purposes only as per the GooAQ license.
:::
::::
:::::::::::::::::

:::::: {#A5 .section .ltx_appendix}
## [Appendix E ]{.ltx_tag .ltx_tag_appendix}Datasets in BEIR {#appendix-e-datasets-in-beir .ltx_title .ltx_title_appendix}

::: {#A5.p1 .ltx_para}
Table [[8]{.ltx_text
.ltx_ref_tag}](#A5.T8 "Table 8 ‣ Appendix E Datasets in BEIR ‣ ColBERTv2:
Effective and Efficient Retrieval via Lightweight Late Interaction"){.ltx_ref}
lists the BEIR datasets we used in our evaluation, including their
respective license information as well as the numbers of documents as
well as the number of test set queries. We refer to [Thakur et al.
2021](#bib.bib58){.ltx_ref} for a more detailed description of each of
the datasets.
:::

::: {#A5.p2 .ltx_para}
Our Touché evaluation uses an updated version of the data in BEIR, which
we use for evaluating the models we run (i.e., ColBERTv2 and RocketQAv2)
as well as SPLADEv2.
:::
